It jumps from contract work, to whether the mortgage is going to be okay, to whether I’m still seen as credible, to whether a recruiter not replying to an email means they’ve decided I’m not worth considering anymore. A delayed response can feel like proof that my whole career is closing down, even though part of me knows that sounds extreme when I say it.\n\nSo yes, I do feel self-conscious, but it’s tied up with this bigger worry that people are judging where I’ve ended up and that I’m losing my place professionally.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms when these thoughts come up, like restlessness, trouble sleeping, muscle tension, or feeling fatigued even when you haven't done much?\n\nPatient: Yes. The biggest thing is this constant wound-up feeling. It’s like my body is bracing for bad news even when nothing is actually happening. I’ll be sitting at my desk or on a call and my shoulders are just locked up near my ears, and I don’t even notice it until they start aching or I realize I’ve been sitting there completely tense for who knows how long.\n\nAnd I pace a lot. Around the house, between calls, sometimes just from one room to another without really needing anything. Or I’ll be on a work call and my leg is bouncing the entire time under the desk. It’s embarrassing because I’m not always responding to an actual crisis—it’s more like my body is acting as if one is about to arrive. I feel keyed up most of the day, like I’m waiting for the next email or phone call to confirm something bad.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that keep coming back, even when you try not to think about them, and do these thoughts feel uncontrollable or cause you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s honestly the main reason I came in. It feels like once a worry gets started, I can’t just… put it back down.\n\nIt’ll start with one thing that should be pretty ordinary, like a client not getting back to me. And I can tell myself, “People are busy, maybe they haven’t seen it,” but that doesn’t really stop it. Within a few minutes I’m already imagining that they’re unhappy with my work, that the contract is going to fall apart, that if that happens I’m going to fall behind financially, and then I’m thinking about divorce payments and whether I can keep up with everything.\n\nAnd then it goes even bigger than that. It turns into this whole story in my head that I’m not going to get back into a real leadership role again, that this is where I’m stuck now, that people have already decided I’m not the person they would trust with responsibility anymore. I know, sitting here, that one unanswered message doesn’t logically mean all of that. But when it’s happening, it feels like my mind is building the case against me faster than I can argue with it.\n\nSo yes, the thoughts keep coming back. I try to push them away or reason with them, but they don’t really let go. It’s distressing because it feels like I’m not in control of my own thinking.\n\nDoctor: Do these recurring thoughts ever feel intrusive or unwanted, like they don’t really belong to you or that you wouldn’t choose to think them if you could control it?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, they feel like my thoughts in the sense that they’re happening in my own head, but they don’t feel like thoughts I’m choosing. I wouldn’t sit down and decide to think this way. That’s actually a big part of why I came in—I can’t shut my brain off anymore.\n\nIt starts with something small, or at least something that should be manageable, like a client not responding to an email or a message. And I’ll tell myself, okay, they’re busy, people don’t answer immediately, that doesn’t mean anything. But it’s like that explanation doesn’t stick. Within minutes I’m thinking, “They’re unhappy with me, I’m going to lose the contract,” and then it jumps to, “If I lose the contract, I’m going to fall behind on divorce payments,” and then somehow I’m already at, “I’m never going to get back into a real leadership role again.”\n\nAnd I don’t want to be thinking that. It feels intrusive in the sense that it barges in and takes over. I can be trying to work or trying to focus on something normal, and there it is, this whole chain of worst-case scenarios. I know I wouldn’t choose it if I had more control over it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to do certain things over and over again, like checking something repeatedly, organizing things in a particular way, or thinking specific thoughts to try to reduce the anxiety that comes with these worries?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, exactly. I don’t think I have a specific ritual or a rule I have to follow, like things needing to be lined up a certain way or touching something a certain number of times. At least, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nI do check things sometimes, but it feels more like trying to reassure myself than something I’m compelled to do in a formal way, if that makes sense. Like I might look back at an email I sent to make sure I didn’t say something wrong, or reread a message because I’m worried I misunderstood it. But I don’t know that it’s the kind of thing where I feel like I have to do it a set number of times or in a specific pattern.\n\nAnd with the thoughts, I do try to talk myself down, but it’s not like there’s a phrase I repeat that makes the anxiety go away. I wish there were, honestly. It’s more like I’m arguing with myself in circles. So… no, I don’t think I have those kinds of repeated behaviors in the way you’re asking, unless I’m misunderstanding what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself doing mental exercises, like silently repeating words, counting, or trying to replace bad thoughts with good ones, in an attempt to manage the anxiety when these worries come up?\n\nPatient: Not really in that specific way, no. I don’t think I sit there silently counting or repeating a word to myself or anything like that.\n\nI do sometimes try to replace the thought, I guess, but it’s not like a structured exercise. It’s more like I’m trying to reason with myself: “You don’t actually know that,” or “Wait until they respond,” or “Don’t jump ten steps ahead.” But then I end up going back and forth with the worry, almost like I’m debating it, and it doesn’t really calm me down in any reliable way.\n\nSo if you mean a ritual, or a specific mental thing I feel I have to do to neutralize the thought, I don’t think so. I haven’t noticed anything like counting or repeating phrases. It’s more just… trying to talk myself out of it and getting stuck in the same loop anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel driven to perform certain actions or behaviors, even if you don’t want to, because not doing them makes your anxiety worse?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in the sense of feeling like I have to perform an action or something bad will happen if I don’t. I mean, I do things to try to make the anxiety go down, but it’s more ordinary reassurance-seeking, I guess, rather than a behavior I feel forced into.\n\nLike if I’m worried about something, I might want to check an email again or look for some sign that I didn’t mess something up, but I don’t have this feeling of, “I must do this exact thing or I won’t be okay.” It’s more that sitting with the uncertainty is really uncomfortable, so I try to relieve it somehow. But I’m not sure I’d call that being driven to perform a behavior.\n\nI guess the closest thing is that if I don’t do anything, the anxiety can keep building and I get stuck thinking about it. But I don’t have a specific action I’m compelled to do, if that’s what you mean. It’s more like I get pulled into trying to figure things out in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself mentally going over the same thoughts or scenarios repeatedly in your head, like replaying a conversation or mentally checking for mistakes, even if you don’t act them out physically?\n\nPatient: Yes. That part, yes. That’s actually a big part of why I came in, because I can’t seem to shut my brain off anymore.\n\nIt’ll start with one thing that might be totally ordinary, like a client not responding to an email. And instead of just thinking, “Okay, they’re busy,” my mind starts running the whole chain. I’ll replay what I wrote, wonder if I sounded wrong, wonder if I missed something, and then within a few minutes I’m not just thinking about the email anymore. I’m thinking, “What if they’re unhappy, what if we lose the contract, what if this reflects badly on me, what if I fall behind financially,” and then it keeps going into how I’m going to manage divorce payments and whether I’m ever going to get back into a real leadership role again.\n\nAnd I know, on some level, that I’m jumping way ahead. I can hear how extreme it sounds when I say it out loud. But in the moment it feels like I have to keep running through it, like if I think hard enough I’ll find the thing I missed or prevent the bad outcome somehow. But I don’t actually get anywhere. It’s just the same scenarios over and over, and I end up exhausted from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself doing anything repeatedly—like checking, thinking, or behaving in a certain way—because you feel that if you don’t, something bad might happen or your anxiety will get even worse?\n\nPatient: Yes, but it’s more the thinking part than doing something with my hands. I don’t really have, like, a repeated behavior I’m acting out over and over, but I do get caught in this loop where it feels like if I stop thinking about it, I’m missing something important and then something bad is going to happen.\n\nThat’s honestly a big part of why I came in. I just can’t shut my brain off anymore. One concern will start it—like a client doesn’t respond to an email—and within minutes I’ve gone from, “Maybe they’re busy,” to, “Maybe I wrote something wrong, maybe they’re unhappy, maybe we’re going to lose the contract.” And then it keeps expanding. Suddenly I’m thinking about falling behind on divorce payments, and whether I’m ever going to get back into a real leadership role, and what that says about me, and it just keeps going.\n\nI know it sounds like I’m jumping way ahead, and I can sometimes recognize that after the fact, but in the moment it feels like I have to keep thinking it through. Like if I don’t keep going over it, I won’t be prepared, or I’ll miss the one thing that could prevent everything from falling apart. But it doesn’t really solve anything. It just makes me more wound up.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself mentally going over and over specific thoughts, trying to neutralize them with other thoughts, or silently repeating certain words or numbers in your head to try to reduce the anxiety or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Not really with words or numbers, no. I don’t have a phrase I repeat or a number pattern or anything like that. It’s not like I’m trying to cancel out a thought with another specific thought.\n\nBut I do go over things in my head again and again, and that’s honestly why I came in. I can’t shut my brain off anymore. It’ll start with one concern, like a client not responding to an email, and then within minutes I’m running through everything that could mean. Maybe I wrote something wrong, maybe they’re upset, maybe we’ll lose the contract. And then it doesn’t stay there—it turns into, “If we lose that, how am I going to keep up with divorce payments?” and then, “What if I never get back into a real leadership role?” and it just keeps branching out.\n\nSo I’m not silently repeating words to prevent something bad, but I am mentally reviewing and reviewing, like if I can just think through every angle, I’ll somehow get control of it or stop the worst thing from happening. It doesn’t really work, though. It just keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that you absolutely must perform a certain mental act or behavior in order to feel that things are under control, even if you don’t really want to do it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, but it’s not like a ritual exactly. It’s more that I feel like I have to keep thinking it through. Like if I don’t keep turning it over in my head, then I’m being careless or I’m letting something slip.\n\nThat’s really why I came in. I can’t shut my brain off anymore. It’ll start with one thing, like a client not replying to an email, and I know logically there could be a dozen normal reasons for that. But my mind doesn’t stay there. Within minutes I’m reviewing what I wrote, wondering if I said something wrong, wondering if they’re unhappy, and then I’m already picturing losing the contract. And then it jumps to, “If that happens, how am I going to keep up with divorce payments?” and then, “What if I never get back into a real leadership role?” It just keeps going.\n\nSo yes, there’s this feeling that I have to mentally run the whole thing out in order to feel even a little bit in control. I don’t want to do it. It doesn’t feel productive. But stopping feels almost irresponsible, like I’m leaving myself exposed to something bad happening.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

It doesn’t really work, though. It just keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that you absolutely must perform a certain mental act or behavior in order to feel that things are under control, even if you don’t really want to do it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, but it’s not like a ritual exactly. I can’t shut my brain off anymore. It’ll start with one thing, like a client not replying to an email, and I know logically there could be a dozen normal reasons for that. And then it jumps to, “If that happens, how am I going to keep up with divorce payments?” and then, “What if I never get back into a real leadership role?” It just keeps going.\n\nSo yes, there’s this feeling that I have to mentally run the whole thing out in order to feel even a little bit in control. I don’t want to do it. It doesn’t feel productive. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, mentally more than physically. I don’t do something like touch a doorknob a certain number of times or anything like that, but I do feel like I have to keep thinking through every possible outcome, because some part of me believes that if I can just anticipate it, I can prevent it.

That’s really what pushed me to come in. I can’t shut my brain off anymore. One small thing will start it, like a client not responding to an email, and instead of just thinking, “They’re busy,” my mind starts building this whole chain. I’ll think maybe I said something wrong, maybe they’re upset, maybe that means we’re going to lose the contract. And then almost immediately it turns into, “If we lose that contract, how am I going to keep up with the divorce payments?” And then it goes even bigger, like, “What if this proves I’m not getting back into a real leadership role? What if I’m stuck?”

So I guess the thing I’m doing is the worrying itself. It feels like I’m trying to prevent something bad by mentally rehearsing it from every angle, even though I know that doesn’t really make sense and it doesn’t actually stop anything. But stopping feels risky, like if I let it go, that’s when I’ll miss something important.

That’s honestly the main reason I came in. It feels like once a worry gets started, I can’t just… put it back down.\n\nIt’ll start with one thing that should be pretty ordinary, like a client not getting back to me. It turns into this whole story in my head that I’m not going to get back into a real leadership role again, that this is where I’m stuck now, that people have already decided I’m not the person they would trust with responsibility anymore. I know, sitting here, that one unanswered message doesn’t logically mean all of that. I try to push them away or reason with them, but they don’t really let go. I mean, they feel like my thoughts in the sense that they’re happening in my own head, but they don’t feel like thoughts I’m choosing. I wouldn’t sit down and decide to think this way. That’s actually a big part of why I came in—I can’t shut my brain off anymore.\n\nIt starts with something small, or at least something that should be manageable, like a client not responding to an email or a message. And I’ll tell myself, okay, they’re busy, people don’t answer immediately, that doesn’t mean anything. But it’s like that explanation doesn’t stick. Within minutes I’m thinking, “They’re unhappy with me, I’m going to lose the contract,” and then it jumps to, “If I lose the contract, I’m going to fall behind on divorce payments,” and then somehow I’m already at, “I’m never going to get back into a real leadership role again.”\n\nAnd I don’t want to be thinking that. It feels intrusive in the sense that it barges in and takes over. I can be trying to work or trying to focus on something normal, and there it is, this whole chain of worst-case scenarios. I know I wouldn’t choose it if I had more control over it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to do certain things over and over again, like checking something repeatedly, organizing things in a particular way, or thinking specific thoughts to try to reduce the anxiety that comes with these worries?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, exactly. I don’t think I have a specific ritual or a rule I have to follow, like things needing to be lined up a certain way or touching something a certain number of times. At least, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nI do check things sometimes, but it feels more like trying to reassure myself than something I’m compelled to do in a formal way, if that makes sense. Like I might look back at an email I sent to make sure I didn’t say something wrong, or reread a message because I’m worried I misunderstood it. I don’t think I sit there silently counting or repeating a word to myself or anything like that.\n\nI do sometimes try to replace the thought, I guess, but it’s not like a structured exercise. It’s more like I’m trying to reason with myself: “You don’t actually know that,” or “Wait until they respond,” or “Don’t jump ten steps ahead.” But then I end up going back and forth with the worry, almost like I’m debating it, and it doesn’t really calm me down in any reliable way.\n\nSo if you mean a ritual, or a specific mental thing I feel I have to do to neutralize the thought, I don’t think so. A delayed response can feel like proof that my whole career is closing down, even though part of me knows that sounds extreme when I say it.\n\nSo yes, I do feel self-conscious, but it’s tied up with this bigger worry that people are judging where I’ve ended up and that I’m losing my place professionally.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms when these thoughts come up, like restlessness, trouble sleeping, muscle tension, or feeling fatigued even when you haven't done much?\n\nPatient: Yes. The biggest thing is this constant wound-up feeling. It’s like my body is bracing for bad news even when nothing is actually happening. I’ll be sitting at my desk or on a call and my shoulders are just locked up near my ears, and I don’t even notice it until they start aching or I realize I’ve been sitting there completely tense for who knows how long.\n\nAnd I pace a lot. Around the house, between calls, sometimes just from one room to another without really needing anything. Or I’ll be on a work call and my leg is bouncing the entire time under the desk. It’s embarrassing because I’m not always responding to an actual crisis—it’s more like my body is acting as if one is about to arrive. I feel keyed up most of the day, like I’m waiting for the next email or phone call to confirm something bad.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that keep coming back, even when you try not to think about them, and do these thoughts feel uncontrollable or cause you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s honestly the main reason I came in. It feels like once a worry gets started, I can’t just… put it back down.\n\nIt’ll start with one thing that should be pretty ordinary, like a client not getting back to me. And I can tell myself, “People are busy, maybe they haven’t seen it,” but that doesn’t really stop it. Within a few minutes I’m already imagining that they’re unhappy with my work, that the contract is going to fall apart, that if that happens I’m going to fall behind financially, and then I’m thinking about divorce payments and whether I can keep up with everything.\n\nAnd then it goes even bigger than that. It turns into this whole story in my head that I’m not going to get back into a real leadership role again, that this is where I’m stuck now, that people have already decided I’m not the person they would trust with responsibility anymore. I know, sitting here, that one unanswered message doesn’t logically mean all of that. But when it’s happening, it feels like my mind is building the case against me faster than I can argue with it.\n\nSo yes, the thoughts keep coming back. I try to push them away or reason with them, but they don’t really let go. It’s distressing because it feels like I’m not in control of my own thinking.\n\nDoctor: Do these recurring thoughts ever feel intrusive or unwanted, like they don’t really belong to you or that you wouldn’t choose to think them if you could control it?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, they feel like my thoughts in the sense that they’re happening in my own head, but they don’t feel like thoughts I’m choosing. I wouldn’t sit down and decide to think this way. That’s actually a big part of why I came in—I can’t shut my brain off anymore.\n\nIt starts with something small, or at least something that should be manageable, like a client not responding to an email or a message. And I’ll tell myself, okay, they’re busy, people don’t answer immediately, that doesn’t mean anything. But it’s like that explanation doesn’t stick. Within minutes I’m thinking, “They’re unhappy with me, I’m going to lose the contract,” and then it jumps to, “If I lose the contract, I’m going to fall behind on divorce payments,” and then somehow I’m already at, “I’m never going to get back into a real leadership role again.”\n\nAnd I don’t want to be thinking that. It feels intrusive in the sense that it barges in and takes over. I can be trying to work or trying to focus on something normal, and there it is, this whole chain of worst-case scenarios. I know I wouldn’t choose it if I had more control over it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to do certain things over and over again, like checking something repeatedly, organizing things in a particular way, or thinking specific thoughts to try to reduce the anxiety that comes with these worries?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, exactly. I don’t think I have a specific ritual or a rule I have to follow, like things needing to be lined up a certain way or touching something a certain number of times. At least, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nI do check things sometimes, but it feels more like trying to reassure myself than something I’m compelled to do in a formal way, if that makes sense. Like I might look back at an email I sent to make sure I didn’t say something wrong, or reread a message because I’m worried I misunderstood it. But I don’t know that it’s the kind of thing where I feel like I have to do it a set number of times or in a specific pattern.\n\nAnd with the thoughts, I do try to talk myself down, but it’s not like there’s a phrase I repeat that makes the anxiety go away. I wish there were, honestly. It’s more like I’m arguing with myself in circles. So… no, I don’t think I have those kinds of repeated behaviors in the way you’re asking, unless I’m misunderstanding what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself doing mental exercises, like silently repeating words, counting, or trying to replace bad thoughts with good ones, in an attempt to manage the anxiety when these worries come up?\n\nPatient: Not really in that specific way, no. I don’t think I sit there silently counting or repeating a word to myself or anything like that.\n\nI do sometimes try to replace the thought, I guess, but it’s not like a structured exercise. It’s more like I’m trying to reason with myself: “You don’t actually know that,” or “Wait until they respond,” or “Don’t jump ten steps ahead.” But then I end up going back and forth with the worry, almost like I’m debating it, and it doesn’t really calm me down in any reliable way.\n\nSo if you mean a ritual, or a specific mental thing I feel I have to do to neutralize the thought, I don’t think so. I haven’t noticed anything like counting or repeating phrases. It’s more just… trying to talk myself out of it and getting stuck in the same loop anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel driven to perform certain actions or behaviors, even if you don’t want to, because not doing them makes your anxiety worse?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in the sense of feeling like I have to perform an action or something bad will happen if I don’t. I mean, I do things to try to make the anxiety go down, but it’s more ordinary reassurance-seeking, I guess, rather than a behavior I feel forced into.\n\nLike if I’m worried about something, I might want to check an email again or look for some sign that I didn’t mess something up, but I don’t have this feeling of, “I must do this exact thing or I won’t be okay.” It’s more that sitting with the uncertainty is really uncomfortable, so I try to relieve it somehow. But I’m not sure I’d call that being driven to perform a behavior.\n\nI guess the closest thing is that if I don’t do anything, the anxiety can keep building and I get stuck thinking about it. But I don’t have a specific action I’m compelled to do, if that’s what you mean. It’s more like I get pulled into trying to figure things out in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself mentally going over the same thoughts or scenarios repeatedly in your head, like replaying a conversation or mentally checking for mistakes, even if you don’t act them out physically?\n\nPatient: Yes. That part, yes. That’s actually a big part of why I came in, because I can’t seem to shut my brain off anymore.\n\nIt’ll start with one thing that might be totally ordinary, like a client not responding to an email. And instead of just thinking, “Okay, they’re busy,” my mind starts running the whole chain. I’ll replay what I wrote, wonder if I sounded wrong, wonder if I missed something, and then within a few minutes I’m not just thinking about the email anymore. I’m thinking, “What if they’re unhappy, what if we lose the contract, what if this reflects badly on me, what if I fall behind financially,” and then it keeps going into how I’m going to manage divorce payments and whether I’m ever going to get back into a real leadership role again.\n\nAnd I know, on some level, that I’m jumping way ahead. I can hear how extreme it sounds when I say it out loud. But in the moment it feels like I have to keep running through it, like if I think hard enough I’ll find the thing I missed or prevent the bad outcome somehow. But I don’t actually get anywhere. It’s just the same scenarios over and over, and I end up exhausted from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself doing anything repeatedly—like checking, thinking, or behaving in a certain way—because you feel that if you don’t, something bad might happen or your anxiety will get even worse?\n\nPatient: Yes, but it’s more the thinking part than doing something with my hands. I don’t really have, like, a repeated behavior I’m acting out over and over, but I do get caught in this loop where it feels like if I stop thinking about it, I’m missing something important and then something bad is going to happen.\n\nThat’s honestly a big part of why I came in. I just can’t shut my brain off anymore. One concern will start it—like a client doesn’t respond to an email—and within minutes I’ve gone from, “Maybe they’re busy,” to, “Maybe I wrote something wrong, maybe they’re unhappy, maybe we’re going to lose the contract.” And then it keeps expanding. Suddenly I’m thinking about falling behind on divorce payments, and whether I’m ever going to get back into a real leadership role, and what that says about me, and it just keeps going.\n\nI know it sounds like I’m jumping way ahead, and I can sometimes recognize that after the fact, but in the moment it feels like I have to keep thinking it through. Like if I don’t keep going over it, I won’t be prepared, or I’ll miss the one thing that could prevent everything from falling apart. But it doesn’t really solve anything. It just makes me more wound up.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself mentally going over and over specific thoughts, trying to neutralize them with other thoughts, or silently repeating certain words or numbers in your head to try to reduce the anxiety or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Not really with words or numbers, no. I don’t have a phrase I repeat or a number pattern or anything like that. It’s not like I’m trying to cancel out a thought with another specific thought.\n\nBut I do go over things in my head again and again, and that’s honestly why I came in. I can’t shut my brain off anymore. It’ll start with one concern, like a client not responding to an email, and then within minutes I’m running through everything that could mean. Maybe I wrote something wrong, maybe they’re upset, maybe we’ll lose the contract. And then it doesn’t stay there—it turns into, “If we lose that, how am I going to keep up with divorce payments?” and then, “What if I never get back into a real leadership role?” and it just keeps branching out.\n\nSo I’m not silently repeating words to prevent something bad, but I am mentally reviewing and reviewing, like if I can just think through every angle, I’ll somehow get control of it or stop the worst thing from happening. It doesn’t really work, though. It just keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that you absolutely must perform a certain mental act or behavior in order to feel that things are under control, even if you don’t really want to do it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, but it’s not like a ritual exactly. It’s more that I feel like I have to keep thinking it through. Like if I don’t keep turning it over in my head, then I’m being careless or I’m letting something slip.\n\nThat’s really why I came in. I can’t shut my brain off anymore. It’ll start with one thing, like a client not replying to an email, and I know logically there could be a dozen normal reasons for that. But my mind doesn’t stay there. Within minutes I’m reviewing what I wrote, wondering if I said something wrong, wondering if they’re unhappy, and then I’m already picturing losing the contract. And then it jumps to, “If that happens, how am I going to keep up with divorce payments?” and then, “What if I never get back into a real leadership role?” It just keeps going.\n\nSo yes, there’s this feeling that I have to mentally run the whole thing out in order to feel even a little bit in control. I don’t want to do it. It doesn’t feel productive. But stopping feels almost irresponsible, like I’m leaving myself exposed to something bad happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever do anything—whether physically or mentally—because you feel it will prevent something bad from happening, even if you know it might not make much sense?\n\nPatient: Yes, mentally more than physically. I don’t do something like touch a doorknob a certain number of times or anything like that, but I do feel like I have to keep thinking through every possible outcome, because some part of me believes that if I can just anticipate it, I can prevent it.\n\nThat’s really what pushed me to come in. I can’t shut my brain off anymore. One small thing will start it, like a client not responding to an email, and instead of just thinking, “They’re busy,” my mind starts building this whole chain. I’ll think maybe I said something wrong, maybe they’re upset, maybe that means we’re going to lose the contract. And then almost immediately it turns into, “If we lose that contract, how am I going to keep up with the divorce payments?” And then it goes even bigger, like, “What if this proves I’m not getting back into a real leadership role? What if I’m stuck?”\n\nSo I guess the thing I’m doing is the worrying itself. It feels like I’m trying to prevent something bad by mentally rehearsing it from every angle, even though I know that doesn’t really make sense and it doesn’t actually stop anything. At work, I’ll lose huge chunks of time because I’m stuck trying to anticipate every possible thing that could go wrong before I send something or respond to someone. I’ve delayed client deliverables because I keep revisiting them, thinking, “What if this is the wrong tone? What if they push back? What if this creates a bigger problem?” And by the time I’ve gone through all of that, I’ve either missed the window I wanted to hit or I’m rushing at the end, which makes me feel even worse.

I’ve also missed networking follow-ups, which is… embarrassing, honestly, because I know those things matter. I’ll meet someone or have a conversation where I should send a quick note afterward, and then I start overthinking what to say, whether I waited too long, whether it’ll seem desperate or awkward, and then suddenly days have gone by and I haven’t done it at all.

And outside of work, I’ve pulled back from people. I used to play soccer and see friends more, but I’ve been avoiding it because I feel tense and irritable, and I’m embarrassed by that. I don’t want to show up and be the guy who’s distracted or snapping at people over nothing. So yes, it’s affecting my work and relationships, and it feels like my world is getting smaller because of it.

I don’t think I have a specific ritual or a rule I have to follow, like things needing to be lined up a certain way or touching something a certain number of times. I wish there were, honestly. It’s more like I’m arguing with myself in circles. I don’t think I sit there silently counting or repeating a word to myself or anything like that.\n\nI do sometimes try to replace the thought, I guess, but it’s not like a structured exercise. I don’t really have, like, a repeated behavior I’m acting out over and over, but I do get caught in this loop where it feels like if I stop thinking about it, I’m missing something important and then something bad is going to happen.\n\nThat’s honestly a big part of why I came in. Like if I don’t keep going over it, I won’t be prepared, or I’ll miss the one thing that could prevent everything from falling apart. But it doesn’t really solve anything. It just makes me more wound up.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself mentally going over and over specific thoughts, trying to neutralize them with other thoughts, or silently repeating certain words or numbers in your head to try to reduce the anxiety or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Not really with words or numbers, no. I don’t have a phrase I repeat or a number pattern or anything like that. It’s not like I’m trying to cancel out a thought with another specific thought.\n\nBut I do go over things in my head again and again, and that’s honestly why I came in. It doesn’t really work, though. It just keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that you absolutely must perform a certain mental act or behavior in order to feel that things are under control, even if you don’t really want to do it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, but it’s not like a ritual exactly. It’s more that I feel like I have to keep thinking it through. Like if I don’t keep turning it over in my head, then I’m being careless or I’m letting something slip.\n\nThat’s really why I came in. I can’t shut my brain off anymore. It’ll start with one thing, like a client not replying to an email, and I know logically there could be a dozen normal reasons for that. And then it jumps to, “If that happens, how am I going to keep up with divorce payments?” and then, “What if I never get back into a real leadership role?” It just keeps going.\n\nSo yes, there’s this feeling that I have to mentally run the whole thing out in order to feel even a little bit in control. I don’t want to do it. It doesn’t feel productive. But stopping feels almost irresponsible, like I’m leaving myself exposed to something bad happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever do anything—whether physically or mentally—because you feel it will prevent something bad from happening, even if you know it might not make much sense?\n\nPatient: Yes, mentally more than physically. I don’t do something like touch a doorknob a certain number of times or anything like that, but I do feel like I have to keep thinking through every possible outcome, because some part of me believes that if I can just anticipate it, I can prevent it.\n\nThat’s really what pushed me to come in. One concern will start it—like a client doesn’t respond to an email—and within minutes I’ve gone from, “Maybe they’re busy,” to, “Maybe I wrote something wrong, maybe they’re unhappy, maybe we’re going to lose the contract.” And then it keeps expanding. Suddenly I’m thinking about falling behind on divorce payments, and whether I’m ever going to get back into a real leadership role, and what that says about me, and it just keeps going.\n\nI know it sounds like I’m jumping way ahead, and I can sometimes recognize that after the fact, but in the moment it feels like I have to keep thinking it through. Like if I don’t keep going over it, I won’t be prepared, or I’ll miss the one thing that could prevent everything from falling apart. But it doesn’t really solve anything. It just makes me more wound up.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself mentally going over and over specific thoughts, trying to neutralize them with other thoughts, or silently repeating certain words or numbers in your head to try to reduce the anxiety or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Not really with words or numbers, no. I don’t have a phrase I repeat or a number pattern or anything like that. It’s not like I’m trying to cancel out a thought with another specific thought.\n\nBut I do go over things in my head again and again, and that’s honestly why I came in. I can’t shut my brain off anymore. It’ll start with one concern, like a client not responding to an email, and then within minutes I’m running through everything that could mean. Maybe I wrote something wrong, maybe they’re upset, maybe we’ll lose the contract. And then it doesn’t stay there—it turns into, “If we lose that, how am I going to keep up with divorce payments?” and then, “What if I never get back into a real leadership role?” and it just keeps branching out.\n\nSo I’m not silently repeating words to prevent something bad, but I am mentally reviewing and reviewing, like if I can just think through every angle, I’ll somehow get control of it or stop the worst thing from happening. It doesn’t really work, though. It just keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that you absolutely must perform a certain mental act or behavior in order to feel that things are under control, even if you don’t really want to do it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, but it’s not like a ritual exactly. It’s more that I feel like I have to keep thinking it through. Like if I don’t keep turning it over in my head, then I’m being careless or I’m letting something slip.\n\nThat’s really why I came in. I can’t shut my brain off anymore. It’ll start with one thing, like a client not replying to an email, and I know logically there could be a dozen normal reasons for that. But my mind doesn’t stay there. Within minutes I’m reviewing what I wrote, wondering if I said something wrong, wondering if they’re unhappy, and then I’m already picturing losing the contract. And then it jumps to, “If that happens, how am I going to keep up with divorce payments?” and then, “What if I never get back into a real leadership role?” It just keeps going.\n\nSo yes, there’s this feeling that I have to mentally run the whole thing out in order to feel even a little bit in control. I don’t want to do it. It doesn’t feel productive. But stopping feels almost irresponsible, like I’m leaving myself exposed to something bad happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever do anything—whether physically or mentally—because you feel it will prevent something bad from happening, even if you know it might not make much sense?\n\nPatient: Yes, mentally more than physically. I don’t do something like touch a doorknob a certain number of times or anything like that, but I do feel like I have to keep thinking through every possible outcome, because some part of me believes that if I can just anticipate it, I can prevent it.\n\nThat’s really what pushed me to come in. I can’t shut my brain off anymore. One small thing will start it, like a client not responding to an email, and instead of just thinking, “They’re busy,” my mind starts building this whole chain. I’ll think maybe I said something wrong, maybe they’re upset, maybe that means we’re going to lose the contract. And then almost immediately it turns into, “If we lose that contract, how am I going to keep up with the divorce payments?” And then it goes even bigger, like, “What if this proves I’m not getting back into a real leadership role? What if I’m stuck?”\n\nSo I guess the thing I’m doing is the worrying itself. It feels like I’m trying to prevent something bad by mentally rehearsing it from every angle, even though I know that doesn’t really make sense and it doesn’t actually stop anything. But stopping feels risky, like if I let it go, that’s when I’ll miss something important.\n\nDoctor: Do these recurring thoughts interfere with your ability to get things done during the day, or cause you so much distress that it affects your work, relationships, or other important areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Yes. Definitely. It’s not just unpleasant in my head anymore—it’s getting in the way of actual things I’m supposed to do.\n\nAt work, I’ll lose huge chunks of time because I’m stuck trying to anticipate every possible thing that could go wrong before I send something or respond to someone. I’ve delayed client deliverables because I keep revisiting them, thinking, “What if this is the wrong tone? What if they push back? What if this creates a bigger problem?” And by the time I’ve gone through all of that, I’ve either missed the window I wanted to hit or I’m rushing at the end, which makes me feel even worse.\n\nI’ve also missed networking follow-ups, which is… embarrassing, honestly, because I know those things matter. I’ll meet someone or have a conversation where I should send a quick note afterward, and then I start overthinking what to say, whether I waited too long, whether it’ll seem desperate or awkward, and then suddenly days have gone by and I haven’t done it at all.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve pulled back from people. I used to play soccer and see friends more, but I’ve been avoiding it because I feel tense and irritable, and I’m embarrassed by that. I don’t want to show up and be the guy who’s distracted or snapping at people over nothing. So yes, it’s affecting my work and relationships, and it feels like my world is getting smaller because of it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. I mean, I know I’ve kind of circled around this already, but that’s really the core of it. It’s not like I believe some ritual is going to magically stop something bad from happening. It’s more like… if I don’t keep mentally working the problem, I feel like I’m being reckless.

It starts with one concern—say a client doesn’t respond to an email—and within minutes I’m not just thinking, “Oh, they’re probably busy.” I’m replaying what I wrote, checking the tone in my head, wondering if I missed something, and then suddenly I’m convinced they’re unhappy, then I’m thinking we’ll lose the contract, and then I’m already jumping to how I’d keep up with divorce payments, and then it becomes this bigger thing about whether I’m ever going to get back into a real leadership role.

And the frustrating part is I don’t even fully believe the worrying helps. I can say that out loud. But in the moment, stopping feels dangerous, like if I don’t run through every possible bad outcome, I’ll miss the one thing I could’ve done to prevent it. So I keep doing it, even though it eats up time and just makes me more anxious.

That’s honestly the main reason I came in. It turns into this whole story in my head that I’m not going to get back into a real leadership role again, that this is where I’m stuck now, that people have already decided I’m not the person they would trust with responsibility anymore. I know, sitting here, that one unanswered message doesn’t logically mean all of that. I try to push them away or reason with them, but they don’t really let go. It’s distressing because it feels like I’m not in control of my own thinking.\n\nDoctor: Do these recurring thoughts ever feel intrusive or unwanted, like they don’t really belong to you or that you wouldn’t choose to think them if you could control it?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, they feel like my thoughts in the sense that they’re happening in my own head, but they don’t feel like thoughts I’m choosing. I wouldn’t sit down and decide to think this way. And I’ll tell myself, okay, they’re busy, people don’t answer immediately, that doesn’t mean anything. But it’s like that explanation doesn’t stick. Within minutes I’m thinking, “They’re unhappy with me, I’m going to lose the contract,” and then it jumps to, “If I lose the contract, I’m going to fall behind on divorce payments,” and then somehow I’m already at, “I’m never going to get back into a real leadership role again.”\n\nAnd I don’t want to be thinking that. It feels intrusive in the sense that it barges in and takes over. I can be trying to work or trying to focus on something normal, and there it is, this whole chain of worst-case scenarios. I know I wouldn’t choose it if I had more control over it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to do certain things over and over again, like checking something repeatedly, organizing things in a particular way, or thinking specific thoughts to try to reduce the anxiety that comes with these worries?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, exactly. I don’t think I have a specific ritual or a rule I have to follow, like things needing to be lined up a certain way or touching something a certain number of times. At least, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nI do check things sometimes, but it feels more like trying to reassure myself than something I’m compelled to do in a formal way, if that makes sense. Like I might look back at an email I sent to make sure I didn’t say something wrong, or reread a message because I’m worried I misunderstood it. But I don’t know that it’s the kind of thing where I feel like I have to do it a set number of times or in a specific pattern.\n\nAnd with the thoughts, I do try to talk myself down, but it’s not like there’s a phrase I repeat that makes the anxiety go away. I wish there were, honestly. It’s more like I’m arguing with myself in circles. So… no, I don’t think I have those kinds of repeated behaviors in the way you’re asking, unless I’m misunderstanding what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself doing mental exercises, like silently repeating words, counting, or trying to replace bad thoughts with good ones, in an attempt to manage the anxiety when these worries come up?\n\nPatient: Not really in that specific way, no. I don’t think I sit there silently counting or repeating a word to myself or anything like that.\n\nI do sometimes try to replace the thought, I guess, but it’s not like a structured exercise. It’s more like I’m trying to reason with myself: “You don’t actually know that,” or “Wait until they respond,” or “Don’t jump ten steps ahead.” But then I end up going back and forth with the worry, almost like I’m debating it, and it doesn’t really calm me down in any reliable way.\n\nSo if you mean a ritual, or a specific mental thing I feel I have to do to neutralize the thought, I don’t think so. I haven’t noticed anything like counting or repeating phrases. It’s more just… trying to talk myself out of it and getting stuck in the same loop anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel driven to perform certain actions or behaviors, even if you don’t want to, because not doing them makes your anxiety worse?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in the sense of feeling like I have to perform an action or something bad will happen if I don’t. I mean, I do things to try to make the anxiety go down, but it’s more ordinary reassurance-seeking, I guess, rather than a behavior I feel forced into.\n\nLike if I’m worried about something, I might want to check an email again or look for some sign that I didn’t mess something up, but I don’t have this feeling of, “I must do this exact thing or I won’t be okay.” It’s more that sitting with the uncertainty is really uncomfortable, so I try to relieve it somehow. But I’m not sure I’d call that being driven to perform a behavior.\n\nI guess the closest thing is that if I don’t do anything, the anxiety can keep building and I get stuck thinking about it. But I don’t have a specific action I’m compelled to do, if that’s what you mean. It’s more like I get pulled into trying to figure things out in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself mentally going over the same thoughts or scenarios repeatedly in your head, like replaying a conversation or mentally checking for mistakes, even if you don’t act them out physically?\n\nPatient: Yes. That part, yes. That’s actually a big part of why I came in, because I can’t seem to shut my brain off anymore.\n\nIt’ll start with one thing that might be totally ordinary, like a client not responding to an email. And instead of just thinking, “Okay, they’re busy,” my mind starts running the whole chain. I’ll replay what I wrote, wonder if I sounded wrong, wonder if I missed something, and then within a few minutes I’m not just thinking about the email anymore. I’m thinking, “What if they’re unhappy, what if we lose the contract, what if this reflects badly on me, what if I fall behind financially,” and then it keeps going into how I’m going to manage divorce payments and whether I’m ever going to get back into a real leadership role again.\n\nAnd I know, on some level, that I’m jumping way ahead. I can hear how extreme it sounds when I say it out loud. But in the moment it feels like I have to keep running through it, like if I think hard enough I’ll find the thing I missed or prevent the bad outcome somehow. But I don’t actually get anywhere. It’s just the same scenarios over and over, and I end up exhausted from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself doing anything repeatedly—like checking, thinking, or behaving in a certain way—because you feel that if you don’t, something bad might happen or your anxiety will get even worse?\n\nPatient: Yes, but it’s more the thinking part than doing something with my hands. I don’t really have, like, a repeated behavior I’m acting out over and over, but I do get caught in this loop where it feels like if I stop thinking about it, I’m missing something important and then something bad is going to happen.\n\nThat’s honestly a big part of why I came in. I just can’t shut my brain off anymore. One concern will start it—like a client doesn’t respond to an email—and within minutes I’ve gone from, “Maybe they’re busy,” to, “Maybe I wrote something wrong, maybe they’re unhappy, maybe we’re going to lose the contract.” And then it keeps expanding. Suddenly I’m thinking about falling behind on divorce payments, and whether I’m ever going to get back into a real leadership role, and what that says about me, and it just keeps going.\n\nI know it sounds like I’m jumping way ahead, and I can sometimes recognize that after the fact, but in the moment it feels like I have to keep thinking it through. Like if I don’t keep going over it, I won’t be prepared, or I’ll miss the one thing that could prevent everything from falling apart. But it doesn’t really solve anything. It just makes me more wound up.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself mentally going over and over specific thoughts, trying to neutralize them with other thoughts, or silently repeating certain words or numbers in your head to try to reduce the anxiety or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Not really with words or numbers, no. I don’t have a phrase I repeat or a number pattern or anything like that. It’s not like I’m trying to cancel out a thought with another specific thought.\n\nBut I do go over things in my head again and again, and that’s honestly why I came in. I can’t shut my brain off anymore. It’ll start with one concern, like a client not responding to an email, and then within minutes I’m running through everything that could mean. Maybe I wrote something wrong, maybe they’re upset, maybe we’ll lose the contract. And then it doesn’t stay there—it turns into, “If we lose that, how am I going to keep up with divorce payments?” and then, “What if I never get back into a real leadership role?” and it just keeps branching out.\n\nSo I’m not silently repeating words to prevent something bad, but I am mentally reviewing and reviewing, like if I can just think through every angle, I’ll somehow get control of it or stop the worst thing from happening. It doesn’t really work, though. It just keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that you absolutely must perform a certain mental act or behavior in order to feel that things are under control, even if you don’t really want to do it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, but it’s not like a ritual exactly. It’s more that I feel like I have to keep thinking it through. Like if I don’t keep turning it over in my head, then I’m being careless or I’m letting something slip.\n\nThat’s really why I came in. I can’t shut my brain off anymore. It’ll start with one thing, like a client not replying to an email, and I know logically there could be a dozen normal reasons for that. But my mind doesn’t stay there. Within minutes I’m reviewing what I wrote, wondering if I said something wrong, wondering if they’re unhappy, and then I’m already picturing losing the contract. And then it jumps to, “If that happens, how am I going to keep up with divorce payments?” and then, “What if I never get back into a real leadership role?” It just keeps going.\n\nSo yes, there’s this feeling that I have to mentally run the whole thing out in order to feel even a little bit in control. I don’t want to do it. It doesn’t feel productive. But stopping feels almost irresponsible, like I’m leaving myself exposed to something bad happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever do anything—whether physically or mentally—because you feel it will prevent something bad from happening, even if you know it might not make much sense?\n\nPatient: Yes, mentally more than physically. I don’t do something like touch a doorknob a certain number of times or anything like that, but I do feel like I have to keep thinking through every possible outcome, because some part of me believes that if I can just anticipate it, I can prevent it.\n\nThat’s really what pushed me to come in. I can’t shut my brain off anymore. One small thing will start it, like a client not responding to an email, and instead of just thinking, “They’re busy,” my mind starts building this whole chain. I’ll think maybe I said something wrong, maybe they’re upset, maybe that means we’re going to lose the contract. And then almost immediately it turns into, “If we lose that contract, how am I going to keep up with the divorce payments?” And then it goes even bigger, like, “What if this proves I’m not getting back into a real leadership role? What if I’m stuck?”\n\nSo I guess the thing I’m doing is the worrying itself. It feels like I’m trying to prevent something bad by mentally rehearsing it from every angle, even though I know that doesn’t really make sense and it doesn’t actually stop anything. But stopping feels risky, like if I let it go, that’s when I’ll miss something important.\n\nDoctor: Do these recurring thoughts interfere with your ability to get things done during the day, or cause you so much distress that it affects your work, relationships, or other important areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Yes. Definitely. It’s not just unpleasant in my head anymore—it’s getting in the way of actual things I’m supposed to do.\n\nAt work, I’ll lose huge chunks of time because I’m stuck trying to anticipate every possible thing that could go wrong before I send something or respond to someone. I’ve delayed client deliverables because I keep revisiting them, thinking, “What if this is the wrong tone? What if they push back? What if this creates a bigger problem?” And by the time I’ve gone through all of that, I’ve either missed the window I wanted to hit or I’m rushing at the end, which makes me feel even worse.\n\nI’ve also missed networking follow-ups, which is… embarrassing, honestly, because I know those things matter. I’ll meet someone or have a conversation where I should send a quick note afterward, and then I start overthinking what to say, whether I waited too long, whether it’ll seem desperate or awkward, and then suddenly days have gone by and I haven’t done it at all.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve pulled back from people. I used to play soccer and see friends more, but I’ve been avoiding it because I feel tense and irritable, and I’m embarrassed by that. I don’t want to show up and be the guy who’s distracted or snapping at people over nothing. So yes, it’s affecting my work and relationships, and it feels like my world is getting smaller because of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the need to perform certain mental acts or behaviors, even if they take up a lot of time or you don’t really believe in them, just because you feel it might reduce your anxiety or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I know I’ve kind of circled around this already, but that’s really the core of it. It’s not like I believe some ritual is going to magically stop something bad from happening. It’s more like… if I don’t keep mentally working the problem, I feel like I’m being reckless.\n\nThat’s honestly why I came in. I can’t shut my brain off anymore. It starts with one concern—say a client doesn’t respond to an email—and within minutes I’m not just thinking, “Oh, they’re probably busy.” I’m replaying what I wrote, checking the tone in my head, wondering if I missed something, and then suddenly I’m convinced they’re unhappy, then I’m thinking we’ll lose the contract, and then I’m already jumping to how I’d keep up with divorce payments, and then it becomes this bigger thing about whether I’m ever going to get back into a real leadership role.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is I don’t even fully believe the worrying helps. I can say that out loud. But in the moment, stopping feels dangerous, like if I don’t run through every possible bad outcome, I’ll miss the one thing I could’ve done to prevent it. So I keep doing it, even though it eats up time and just makes me more anxious.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

What if this creates a bigger problem?” And by the time I’ve gone through all of that, I’ve either missed the window I wanted to hit or I’m rushing at the end, which makes me feel even worse.\n\nI’ve also missed networking follow-ups, which is… embarrassing, honestly, because I know those things matter. I’ll meet someone or have a conversation where I should send a quick note afterward, and then I start overthinking what to say, whether I waited too long, whether it’ll seem desperate or awkward, and then suddenly days have gone by and I haven’t done it at all.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve pulled back from people. I used to play soccer and see friends more, but I’ve been avoiding it because I feel tense and irritable, and I’m embarrassed by that. I don’t want to show up and be the guy who’s distracted or snapping at people over nothing. So yes, it’s affecting my work and relationships, and it feels like my world is getting smaller because of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the need to perform certain mental acts or behaviors, even if they take up a lot of time or you don’t really believe in them, just because you feel it might reduce your anxiety or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I know I’ve kind of circled around this already, but that’s really the core of it. It’s not like I believe some ritual is going to magically stop something bad from happening. It’s more like… if I don’t keep mentally working the problem, I feel like I’m being reckless.\n\nThat’s honestly why I came in. I can’t shut my brain off anymore. It starts with one concern—say a client doesn’t respond to an email—and within minutes I’m not just thinking, “Oh, they’re probably busy.” I’m replaying what I wrote, checking the tone in my head, wondering if I missed something, and then suddenly I’m convinced they’re unhappy, then I’m thinking we’ll lose the contract, and then I’m already jumping to how I’d keep up with divorce payments, and then it becomes this bigger thing about whether I’m ever going to get back into a real leadership role.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is I don’t even fully believe the worrying helps. I can say that out loud. But in the moment, stopping feels dangerous, like if I don’t run through every possible bad outcome, I’ll miss the one thing I could’ve done to prevent it. So I keep doing it, even though it eats up time and just makes me more anxious.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. I know I’ve said it a few different ways, but that’s exactly what it feels like. It’s like I’m driven to keep thinking, keep analyzing, keep running scenarios, because if I stop, some part of me feels like that’s when something bad will happen—or at least that I’ll have failed to prevent it.

That’s really why I came in. I can’t shut my brain off anymore. It can start with something small, like a client not responding to an email. And instead of just letting it sit or assuming they’re busy, my mind grabs onto it. Within minutes I’m replaying what I sent, wondering if I said something wrong, imagining they’re unhappy, then jumping to losing the contract. And then it’s not even just about work anymore—it becomes, “What if I fall behind on the divorce payments?” and then, “What if this means I’m never going to get back into a real leadership role?”

Logically, I can hear how extreme that sounds. I don’t actually think worrying for hours is going to control the outcome. But in the moment, not doing it feels almost irresponsible, like I’m leaving something exposed. So I keep going over it, even though it doesn’t solve anything and usually just makes me feel worse.
